Original lyrics that mirror a corpus, without touching the corpus

A large public lyrics corpus of 5,134,856 songs is measured end to end; abstract style profiles are distilled from those measurements; and 240 entirely new songs are written from the profiles alone, in four languages, then judged and checked against the corpus distribution with numbers. No source lyric enters any prompt, and none is shown on this page.

Why the method changed. The original proposal was to take a real song and rewrite it — keep its structure and length, swap the nouns and names. That does not produce copyright-clean text. A line-by-line paraphrase with substitutions is a derivative work: it preserves the selection and arrangement that the copyright actually protects, and every line stays traceable to its source. Instead, everything the corpus contributes here is aggregate — medians over tens of thousands of songs, term-frequency lifts, section-marker rates. The writing model is handed a genre name, a numeric structural brief and a theme category at the level of “longing” or “defiance”, and nothing else. It has never seen a song from the corpus.

1 · The corpus, measured

5,134,856songs
641,337unique artists
6genre tags
85languages
241median words / song
1.76%duplicate lyrics

Every number on this page was computed by streaming the corpus into 26 parquet shards on the parallel filesystem and extracting one feature row per song — word and character counts, line counts, type-token ratio, section markers, pronoun and tense counts, and a hash of the normalised lyric for duplicate detection. Nothing was silently dropped: the filters below are reported, not applied.

2 · Genres and their length distributions

The corpus ships 6 genre tags, and they are the only genre signal in the data. They are very unevenly filled — the largest is 21× the smallest — and one of them, misc, is a catch-all rather than a genre.

Why the mean alone would mislead you. Song length is right-skewed in every genre: a short floor (a lyric cannot be much under 40 words and still be a song) and a very long tail. Across the whole corpus the mean is 303 words but the median is 241 — the mean sits at the 64th percentile, not the middle. The standard deviation (393) is larger than the interquartile range (218), which is the signature of a distribution the normal-curve intuition does not describe. In misc the gap is extreme: mean 868 words against a median of 236, because 12.2% of the rows in that tag are longer than 2,000 words — those are not songs at all. Anything sized from the mean would come out roughly a quarter too long, which is exactly the error the generator had to be corrected for later on this page.

3 · Languages

85 languages are labelled. English dominates at 65.7%, but the non-English half of the corpus is not a rounding error: Spanish, French, Portuguese, Russian, German and Italian each contribute six figures of songs. This is what makes a four-language generation target reasonable rather than artificial.

4 · Structure markers

56.1% of songs carry at least one explicit section marker such as a bracketed [Verse 1] or [Chorus] on its own line. That is a property of how the corpus was transcribed, not of the music, and it varies enormously by genre — from 25.7% in the weakest tag to 79.7% in the strongest. Any generator that mirrors this corpus has to reproduce the convention, not just the words.

marker% of songs containing itmean occurrences / song

chorus21.9%0.555

20.1% of all songs mark a chorus at least twice — the repeat is the single most reliable structural signal in the corpus.

5 · Data quality, honestly

issuerowssharewhat it is
empty lyrics2420.005%zero word tokens after stripping section markers
under 20 words8,0490.157%fragments, placeholders, single-line stubs
over 2,000 words24,8140.483%almost entirely misc: transcripts, poems, speeches, tracklists
duplicate lyric text90,2981.759%byte-identical after whitespace normalisation, under a different title or artist
duplicate artist+title120.000%the corpus was already deduplicated on this key — effectively clean
missing genre tag00.000%none: every row carries a tag
misc tag181,4553.534%carries a tag but not a genre — a catch-all bucket
translation / romanisation bot rows58,9741.149%uploaded by corpus translation accounts; these are renderings of other songs, not works
The tail is real and it is concentrated. 12.2% of the misc tag is over 2,000 words and its 99th percentile is 8,780 words — against 624 for pop and 505 for rock. The longest single row is 20,553 words. Those rows are transcripts, poems and speeches that were filed under a music corpus; they are not songs, and any statistic that averages over misc without saying so is wrong.
The most prolific “artists” are not artists. The ten largest artist buckets are corpus translation and romanisation accounts — the single largest holds 16,325 rows. Two more of the top fifteen are a 19th-century poet and a 19th-century head of state, both sitting in misc. Together the translation and romanisation accounts account for 58,974 rows (1.15%), and they are near-duplicates of other rows in the same corpus by construction — which is a large part of why the exact-duplicate rate is 1.76% despite artist+title being effectively unique.

Largest artist buckets

Names are generalised below: the corpus is referred to only as a large public lyrics corpus, so its platform-specific account names are described rather than quoted.

Artist coverage is extremely long-tailed in the other direction too: 48.0% of artists appear exactly once, while the top 1% of artists account for 23.7% of all rows.

Applying the obvious filters — 20 to 2,000 words, first occurrence of each lyric hash, a tag present — keeps 5,013,513 rows (97.6%). The corpus is in good shape; the problems are concentrated in one tag and one class of uploader.

6 · A 20-genre taxonomy derived from the corpus

A gap between the brief and the data. The brief asks for 20 genres. The corpus supplies 6 tags, and one of them is a catch-all. Rather than invent 20 labels, the six tags were sub-divided from the data: within each tag, an English sample of 60,000 songs was TF-IDF vectorised and k-means clustered, with the number of clusters allocated roughly in proportion to the tag's share of the corpus (a floor of two for the small tags). Each cluster's most over-represented content words — an aggregate lift statistic over tens of thousands of songs, never a song — were then handed to the model together with the cluster's numeric aggregates, and it returned a name, a register, a sentiment arc and three abstract theme categories. Every derived genre therefore has a real member population whose structure can be measured.
One artefact worth recording. A first clustering pass produced clusters that were about punctuation, not genre: some songs use a typographic apostrophe and some an ASCII one, so “don't” tokenises either whole or as a bare “don”, and k-means happily found the transcription convention instead of the content. Normalising the apostrophe and stop-listing contraction debris and section words fixed it, and the second pass produced the clusters below.

7 · From statistics to a style profile

Each derived genre yields three profiles — 20 × 3 = 60 — that differ in two ways. Each takes a different one of the genre's three theme categories, and each is sized at a different point of that genre's own length distribution: one at the median, one at the lower quartile, one at the upper quartile. That matters for the distribution match: profiles all sized at the median would generate a corpus with the right centre and no spread.

The profile is then rendered into a brief. Here is a complete one, exactly as the writing model receives it — this is the entirety of what the corpus contributes:

Genre: Ethereal Folk-Pop — A melancholic and atmospheric style blending folk imagery with pop structures, focusing on nature, mortality, and seasonal cycles. Theme category: Mortal Transience - about 170 words total, laid out over about 28 lines - average line length about 6.0 words - exact section layout (line counts are budgets, keep to them): Verse 1: 12 lines Chorus: 4 lines Verse 2: 12 lines - no section markers; run the sections together as continuous lyric lines - lexical variety: aim for a type-token ratio near 0.45 (moderately varied) - point of view: first-person singular (First-person narrator addressing a collective or abstract audience) - tense: mainly present-tense - register: elevated - emotional arc: Nostalgic reflection transitioning into solemn acceptance of transience - HARD BUDGET: 28 lyric lines in total, 170 words (+/-15%). Do not run long: stop when the layout is filled.

There is no source text in it, and there is nothing in it that could be traced to a particular song. The line-count budget in that brief is the result of a correction described in section 9.

8 · Judge results

A second pass with the same model scores every generated text on five dimensions, each with the same five word-anchored levels — poor, weak, adequate, strong, excellent — and an explicit definition of what each level means for that dimension. The judge is instructed to be hard to impress: “excellent” is reserved for work it would sign. It sees the brief and the candidate, never the corpus. 0 of 240 responses failed to parse.

These are one model's opinions of its own output, which is the weakest kind of evidence on this page. The section below replaces opinion with measurement.

9 · Does it mirror the distribution?

The generated texts are run back through the same feature extractor that measured the corpus, so the two sides are measured identically, and compared per genre on word count, line count, line length, type-token ratio and structure-marker frequency.

0.86rank corr., length vs target
16%median word-count error
7%median line-count error
100%marker-convention compliance
0.516generated median TTR
0.454corpus median TTR
What the first round got wrong, and the fix. Round one gave the model only totals — “about 170 words over about 28 lines”. It tracked the targets in rank order (Spearman 0.80) but ran systematically long: a median word-count error of 20% and a line-count error of 44%, with English overshooting its word budget by a median 27%. Round two replaces the totals with an explicit per-section line budget — Verse 1: 12 lines, Chorus: 4 lines — and a stated hard cap. Word error fell to 16% and line error to 7%, rank correlation with the target rose from 0.80 to 0.86, and the share of texts landing within ±25% of their word budget went from 43% to 75% in English. The judge's structure score moved with it, from 2.38 to 2.87.
And what the fix cost. The correction was not free. In round one the median type-token ratio of the generated set was 0.455 against a corpus median of 0.454 — almost exactly right, unprompted. Round two's shorter, tightly budgeted texts push it to 0.516, 14% above the corpus. That is mechanical rather than stylistic: type-token ratio falls as a text gets longer, so cutting length raises it, and real songs get their low ratios from heavy verbatim repetition — a hook sung eight times — which the model still under-produces. Matching length and matching repetition are in tension, and only one of them was fixed here. Weighting a third round toward explicit repeat counts per chorus would be the obvious next move.

10 · The generated lyrics

All 240 texts, grouped by derived genre, four languages side by side, each with the judge’s five scores and the profile that conditioned it. The four language versions of a profile are independent compositions written to the same brief — they are not translations of one another, and comparing them shows how differently the same structural spec lands in each language.

Ethereal Folk-Pop

parent tag: pop24% of that tag14,186 member songs measuredregister: elevated
A melancholic and atmospheric style blending folk imagery with pop structures, focusing on nature, mortality, and seasonal cycles.

Mortal Transience typical

- about 170 words total, laid out over about 28 lines - average line length about 6.0 words - exact section layout (line counts are budgets, keep to them): Verse 1: 12 lines Chorus: 4 lines Verse 2: 12 lines - no section markers; run the sections together as continuous lyric lines - lexical variety: aim for a type-token ratio near 0.45 (moderately varied) - point of view: first-person singular (First-person narrator addressing a collective or abstract audience) - tense: mainly present-tense - register: elevated - emotional arc: Nostalgic reflection transitioning into solemn acceptance of transience - HARD BUDGET: 28 lyric lines in total, 170 words (+/-15%). Do not run long: stop when the layout is filled.
English
genre fit strong structure weak originality adequate language strong overall weak

Imagery is competent but generic; structure fails to distinguish chorus from verses as required.

The frost climbs up the hollow stalk
Where summer’s heavy bloom once hung
We gather here beneath the gray
To watch the final light recede
The wind strips bare the cedar bough
And carries scent of old decay
I hold my breath against the cold
And map the silence in the air
The river slows its rushing pace
Reflecting clouds in silver sheets
We stand beside the fading day
And name the shadows passing by
The sun descends into the west
Leaving a bruise upon the sky
We cannot keep the golden hour
It slips through fingers like the sand
But there is peace in letting go
In trusting what remains behind
The dark arrives with quiet grace
To wrap the world in restful sleep
We do not fear the closing eye
For every end prepares the start
The roots will wait through winter’s chill
To drink the rain of spring again
We bow our heads in solemn turn
And bless the cycle turning round
The light returns without our call
To touch the waking earth with grace
We accept the brief and burning glow
And let the long night hold us tight
Deutsch
genre fit strong structure weak originality adequate language strong overall weak

parent tag: pop5% of that tag3,281 member songs measuredregister: liturgical
A melodic pop sub-genre characterized by reverent, declarative lyrics focused on divine attributes and communal praise, often featuring anthemic choruses and a blend of modern production with traditional hymn-like structures.

Introspective Melodic Rap

parent tag: rap19% of that tag11,571 member songs measuredregister: conversational
A reflective and emotionally vulnerable sub-genre characterized by slower tempos, heavy use of first-person narration, and lyrical focus on internal psychological states and existential questioning.

Existential uncertainty typical

[Verse 1]

I do not beg for mercy from the sky

I am the breath before the last exhale

The gates of nothing swing their iron jaws
I walk through fire without feeling heat
I am unmade by what I cannot see

The stars have burned out, leaving only black
I hold the void within my open palm
No prayer remains to break the iron spell
My soul unravels thread by silent thread
I find the peace inside the breaking point
I am the dust that settles on the grave

11 · Throughput and cost

The model is served with SGLang 0.5.12 on one JUPITER Booster node — four NVIDIA GH200 GPUs, 95 GB of HBM each, aarch64 Grace hosts. SGLang was chosen over vLLM for two reasons, one architectural and one practical. Architecturally, the model is a hybrid linear/full-attention design (three linear-attention layers to every full-attention layer, 64 layers in total) with a multi-token-prediction head, and SGLang 0.5.12 ships first-class support for that architecture. Practically, a working aarch64 SGLang environment with a CUDA-13 toolchain already existed on this cluster; building vLLM from source for aarch64 against the same architecture would have been a multi-hour gamble for no expected throughput advantage, and the cluster rules here forbid mutating a working environment with pip install.

What was optimised before measuring

  • Four independent replicas, tensor-parallel 1, one per GPU, rather than one TP=4 server. The weights are 29 GB in FP8 and fit in a single 95 GB GPU with room for a large KV cache, so tensor parallelism would only add all-reduce traffic. For throughput-bound batch work four independent schedulers beat one wide one.
  • Thinking disabled via the chat template. This model emits a reasoning block by default; for a generation task whose output is the artefact, that is pure waste. Disabling it removes several hundred tokens of unused output per request.
  • Per-request token budgets scaled to the profile, not a flat ceiling: a request whose brief asks for 170 words gets a different budget from one asking for 480. Requests are then grouped into length buckets so each batch shares a ceiling, which keeps the scheduler's memory planning tight.
  • Context length capped at 8,192 against a model maximum of 262,144. Prompts are ~430 tokens and outputs at most ~2,300; reserving a quarter-million-token context would have cost KV cache for nothing.
  • Prefix caching left on. Every prompt shares a system message and a genre header, so the radix cache serves a real fraction of the prefill for free.

Extrapolation to the full corpus

One original song for every song in the corpus means 5,134,856 generations at the measured full-node rate of 12,082 songs per GPU-hour.

425GPU-hours
106node-hours (4 GPUs)
4node-days
0.3days on 16 nodes

Judging every generation as well roughly adds a further pass. The judge prompt carries the full candidate text so its prefill is larger, but it emits only ~120 tokens against ~450 for generation, so in this run judging 240 texts took 12 s against 59 s to generate them — about 20% of the generation cost. Budget roughly 510 GPU-hours for generation plus judging end to end.

Caveats on these numbers: they are a single node's measurement on a shared cluster, at a fixed prompt shape and a mean output of 506 tokens. A real full-corpus run would also pay model load time on every node (roughly ten minutes here, dominated by reading the weights and the Python environment off the parallel filesystem) and would need checkpointing, which this measurement does not include.

Reproducibility

  • Corpus streamed to parquet shards, one feature row per song; all aggregates computed from those.
  • Genre taxonomy: TF-IDF + MiniBatchKMeans within each corpus tag, 60,000 English songs sampled per tag, seeded.
  • Generation: 60 profiles × 4 languages = 240 texts, temperature 0.95, top-p 0.95, thinking off.
  • Judging: same model, temperature 0.2, five word-anchored levels, 0 parse failures out of 240.
  • Generation wall time for all 240 texts: 59 s (101,082 output tokens). Judging: 12 s.

No lyric from the corpus appears in any prompt on either pass, and none appears on this page.
